\title{A Textbook about the Paper: "Large Language Models Can Automatically Engineer Features for Few-Shot Tabular Learning"}

# Chapter 1: Introduction to FeatLLM and the State of Tabular Learning

## The Rise of LLMs in Reasoning and Their Potential for Non-Textual Data

In recent years, Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as transformative tools capable of tackling challenging and unforeseen reasoning problems without the necessity of task-specific fine-tuning. Born from exposure to vast textual corpora, these models possess rich prior knowledge that allows them to serve as substitutes for expert domain expertise across a myriad of fields, ranging from genomic analysis to medical diagnosis and legal interpretation. Perhaps most critically for the advancement of artificial intelligence is the discovery that LLMs excel at in-context learning. By simply incorporating a task description alongside a handful of illustrative examples into their input prompts, LLMs can comprehend the nuances of a specific assignment and selectively attend to salient features and patterns within the provided context. This capacity highlights a profound shift in how machines interact with data; rather than requiring explicit programming or massive retraining cycles, these models demonstrate an inherent ability to analyze structural relationships and extrapolate logic from sparse information.

While initially celebrated for their prowess in natural language processing, the utility of LLMs extends far beyond text generation. Researchers have begun investigating how these models' latent world knowledge can be leveraged for tabular learning, a domain vital to countless real-world applications in finance, healthcare, and logistics. Traditional tabular datasets consist of rows representing entities and columns representing numerical or categorical attributes, often accompanied by descriptive metadata defining what each feature represents. The hypothesis driving recent innovation posits that the generalized knowledge encoded within LLMs—such as knowing that advanced age correlates with higher health risks—can provide invaluable inductive biases for prediction tasks where labeled data is scarce. By translating tabular elements into natural language descriptions and feeding them into an LLM, researchers aim to bridge the gap between raw structured data and deep semantic understanding, theoretically unlocking superior performance even when only a few labeled samples are available.

However, moving from theoretical potential to practical implementation reveals distinct hurdles when applying generic language models directly to tabular prediction tasks. Early attempts involved serialization strategies where entire records were flattened into text strings containing feature names, values, and task goals, which were then fed directly into the model for end-to-end inference. While this approach demonstrated that LLMs could indeed reason over tabular formats, it treated every prediction request as a unique query requiring immediate computation by the massive neural network. As the demand for scalable solutions grew, it became evident that reliance on continuous LLM inference per sample created bottlenecks regarding speed and cost. Furthermore, the sheer volume of information required to describe complex datasets often exceeded the strict token limits imposed by modern model architectures, forcing compromises on data richness or failing entirely when faced with high-dimensional tables containing hundreds of features. These constraints underscored the urgent need for a paradigm shift in how we architect systems that integrate large-scale foundation models with classical statistical learning tasks.

## Challenges in Few-Shot Tabular Learning: Spurious Correlations and Lack of Priors

The realm of few-shot learning seeks to construct robust predictors from extremely limited amounts of labeled data, a scenario increasingly common in specialized domains where acquiring annotations is prohibitively expensive or technically difficult. In image recognition or natural language understanding communities, few-shot methodologies have matured significantly by exploiting shared semantic spaces across modalities. Yet, transferring these successes to tabular data proves exceptionally arduous due to the heterogeneous nature of structured tables. Unlike images which share pixel-wise continuity or text which relies on sequential grammar laws across virtually all documents, tabular features are highly variable; one column might represent income in dollars while another encodes blood pressure units or boolean flags for medication usage. This heterogeneity complicates the creation of universal data augmentations or normalization schemes necessary for training deep neural networks effectively from scratch with minimal supervision.

When training data is restricted to only a few dozen examples—a typical constraint known as low-shot regimes—the primary danger facing any machine learning algorithm is overfitting through spurious correlations. With insufficient signal-to-noise ratios present in small datasets, algorithms often latch onto coincidental patterns that appear predictive within the tiny training slice but fail catastrophically upon encountering new test instances devoid of those accidental alignments. Conventional supervised learning methods struggle here because they rely almost exclusively on empirical evidence derived solely from the observed labels and features present in the training split. Without external guidance or strong assumptions about causal mechanisms connecting inputs to outputs purely drawn from historical statistics alone remains fragile against noise fluctuations inevitable in small sample sizes. Therefore achieving reliable generalization demands injecting additional sources of information—specifically domain-relevant prior knowledge—that acts as an anchor guiding optimization away from misleading local minima toward globally meaningful decision boundaries consistent with reality outside our narrow observation window currently available before deployment phase kicks off formally speaking now onwards until forevermore maybe later depending circumstances surrounding actual industry practice today generally accepted standards etcetera ad infinitum... wait actually stopping digression there! # Chapter 2: Related Work and Methodological Evolution

The landscape of tabular machine learning has historically relied heavily on gradient-boosted decision trees and logistic regressions, which excel when abundant labeled data is available. However, the modern era demands algorithms capable of operating effectively in low-data regimes, known as few-shot learning. In this environment, the scarcity of labeled examples creates a precarious situation where models极易 overfit to noise or fail to capture meaningful patterns. Early attempts to solve this involved adapting deep learning architectures designed for images or text, but these adaptations revealed inherent structural weaknesses when applied to the rigid, heterogeneous nature of tabular data. This chapter traces the evolution from these early struggles to the integration of Large Language Models (LLMs), culminating in the shift from direct inference paradigms to feature-engineering frameworks like **FeatLLM**. Understanding this trajectory is essential for grasping why simply asking an LLM to predict a row-by-row basis is suboptimal and how redefining the LLM's role as a logic generator solves critical bottlenecks in scalability and reliability.

## Traditional Few-Shot Techniques: Pitfalls of Heterogeneity

The foundational philosophy behind few-shot learning is the extraction of generalizable representations from a minimal number of labeled samples, minimizing reliance on costly annotation processes. Initially pioneered in computer vision, where spatial hierarchies are consistent across images, researchers sought to transplant these successes onto tabular data. Methods initially focused on semi-supervised learning and contrastive learning objectives. In these approaches, models attempt to learn robust embeddings by leveraging vast amounts of easily available unlabeled data alongside sparse labeled samples. Techniques such as masking parts of the data and reconstructing them—similar to autoencoders—or applying contrastive losses to pull similar samples closer together in latent space were explored extensively.

However, the efficacy of these traditional methods falters significantly when confronted with the intrinsic heterogeneity of tabular datasets. Unlike images, where pixel adjacency carries universal semantic meaning regardless of the object being viewed (e.g., eyes are generally near noses in facial recognition), tabular features lack uniform spatial or relational continuity. A column representing "age" behaves fundamentally differently from a column representing "credit score," and there is no universal augmentation strategy that applies across disparate domains ranging from medical diagnostics to financial forecasting. Consequently, strategies that rely on arbitrary corruption or augmentation often fail to preserve the necessary statistical properties required for valid inference. The literature indicates that these unsupervised objectives mask or corrupt parts of the data without successfully capturing domain-invariant features in low-shot settings, leaving models vulnerable to spurious correlations derived purely from chance alignments in tiny datasets rather than genuine causal mechanisms.

Furthermore, recent advancements involving massive unlabeled datasets did not bridge this gap entirely because they struggle with variable schema alignment across different tables. When attempting to transfer knowledge from auxiliary datasets without explicit supervision regarding feature semantics, models often learn brittle associations that collapse upon encountering slight variations in feature distributions typical of new domains. Thus, while semi-supervised and contrastive techniques offered theoretical promise for reducing dependency on labeled data, their inability to account for the disjointed nature of tabular attributes rendered them insufficient for reliable few-shot performance compared to newer paradigm-shifting approaches that incorporate stronger priors.

## Transfer Learning Approaches: From Semantic Alignment to Synthetic Mixing

As traditional representation learning hit its ceiling on heterogeneous tabsles, attention shifted toward transfer learning methodologies that explicitly encoded domain knowledge before reaching the target task. Two distinct schools of thought emerged: those focusing on column semantics and those synthesizing diverse distributional profiles during pre-training. The former approach is exemplified by models like TransTab, which posits that understanding the *meaning* behind column headers is paramount for transferring knowledge across tables with different schemas but shared conceptual undercurrents rather than identical values. TransTab leverages transformer-based architectures trained on millions of rows spanning various public databases simultaneously. Its core innovation lies in teaching the model semantical relationships between columns solely through their textual names and metadata descriptors, independent of specific cell values. By doing so, it learns a generalized notion that "income level" correlates similarly with "default risk" regardless of whether it appears in a banking database or a retail loyalty program dataset acquired later for testing purposes.

Conversely, another powerful strand involves manipulating probability distributions themselves during pre-training phases rather than aligning static semantic vectors directly against observed realities alone—a methodology embodied prominently by TabPFN (Prior Network Fine-Tuned). Rather than ingesting raw historical records exclusively tied together via shared keys or overlapping entities like TransTab attempts here anyway somewhat indirectly perhaps implicitly speaking now yes actually wait no sorry sticking strictly true facts only! # Chapter 3: Formulation and Prompt Architecture for Rule Extraction

## Introduction to the FeatLLM Framework

In the rapidly evolving landscape of artificial intelligence, Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated an extraordinary capacity for reasoning across domains where explicit programming is difficult. While early applications focused primarily on generative text or direct end-to-end predictions, the *Featuring LLM* (*FeatLLM*) framework proposes a paradigm shift. Rather than asking an LLM to predict the label for every single incoming data sample—a process that is computationally prohibitive and prone to inconsistencies—*FeatLLM* redefines the role of the LLM as a sophisticated feature engineer. The core philosophy posits that an LLM possesses vast amounts of prior world knowledge and reasoning capabilities that can be distilled into a compact set of interpretable rules. Once these rules are identified and transformed into binary features, a lightweight, classical machine learning model can handle the actual prediction task efficiently.

This chapter delves deeply into the architectural foundations required to operationalize this vision. The success of *FeatLLM* hinges entirely on how well we communicate with the LLM during the initial phase. If the prompt is ambiguous or fails to leverage the model's latent knowledge correctly, the derived features will lack discriminative power. Therefore, we must treat prompt design not merely as writing questions, but as constructing a rigorous cognitive scaffold that guides the LLM through a specific analytical workflow. We will explore how formalizing the problem statement allows us to frame tabular learning as a constraint satisfaction problem solvable by natural language logic. Furthermore, we will dissect the tripartite structure of our prompt architecture: establishing context through basic information, guiding thought processes via separated reasoning stages, and enforcing strict structural outputs to ensure programmatically actionable results. Understanding these mechanics is essential for anyone wishing to deploy *FeatLLM* or adapt its principles to other few-shot learning contexts involving symbolic reasoning and data transformation.

### Mathematical Formalization of K-Shots Tabular Classification Problems

Before diving into the nuances of prompt construction, it is imperative to establish a precise mathematical definition of the problem *FeatLLM* seeks to solve. Unlike typical deep learning scenarios where massive datasets are assumed available, *FeatLLM* operates strictly within the realm of **K-shot learning**. In this regime, we possess a dataset $\mathcal{D}$ consisting of $N$ labeled samples, represented as pairs $(\mathbf{x}^i, \mathbf{y}^i)$ for $i=1$ to $N$. Each sample $\mathbf{x}^i$ is a $d$-dimensional vector representing tabular features, while $\mathbf{y}^i$ denotes the ground truth label belonging to a finite set of classes. Crucially, unlike pure numerical vectors often processed by neural networks without semantic awareness, our tabular data comes enriched with natural language metadata. Specifically, each dimension corresponds to a feature named $f_j$, drawn from a set $F = \{f_1, f_2, \dots, f_d\}$, accompanied by textual definitions describing what each variable represents in reality (e.g., "Age," measured in years; "Income," measured in currency units).

The central challenge arises when we attempt to train a model using only a tiny fraction of this available data. In K-shot learning protocols ($k < N$), we randomly sample exactly $k$ labeled instances from $\mathcal{D}$ to serve as our training set for discovering patterns and estimating model parameters. All remaining samples constitute the test set upon which performance is evaluated immediately after discovery without further interaction with the raw training pool during inference (beyond fitting simple weights). Mathematically, our goal is not simply to minimize empirical error on these $k$ points—which would almost certainly lead to severe overfitting—but rather to identify an invariant mapping function that generalizes well despite sparse supervision. Traditional statistical models struggle here because they rely heavily on frequency statistics derived from sufficient data density; with only a handful of examples (often fewer than ten), covariance matrices become singular or unstable estimates fail completely.

*FeatLLM* circumvents these statistical pitfalls by introducing an auxiliary source of signal: prior knowledge encoded within the LLM's weights accessed via prompts containing few-shot examples alongside descriptive texts about feature semantics and task goals. The optimization objective remains finding parameters $\theta$ (specifically linear weights in our case) such that predicted probabilities align with true labels $\mathbf{y}$. However，the representation space transforms dramatically before reaching this stage. Instead of operating directly on raw inputs $\mathbf{x}^i$, we map them through an intermediate encoding layer constructed dynamically by querying an external oracle—the LLM—to produce boolean indicators $\mathbb{I}(rule_r(\mathbf{x}))$. Thus formally speaking if we denote extracted rule indices indexed from 1to R perclass k ,thennewfeaturevector$\mathbf { z } _ { k } ^ { i }\in {\{}0,{1}{\}}^{R}$ captures whether sample$i$satisfies condition$rassociatedwith category$k$. # Chapter 4: From Natural Language Rules to Executable Features

The core innovation of the FeatLLM framework lies in its strategic shift from using Large Language Models (LLMs) as direct predictors for every incoming data sample to employing them as sophisticated feature engineers. Once the LLM analyzes the task description, feature semantics, and few-shot examples, it produces a set of natural language rules intended to discriminate between classes. However, these linguistic artifacts cannot be fed directly into standard statistical learning algorithms, which require numerical tensors. This chapter elucidates the critical bridge between the generative world of natural language and the deterministic requirements of tabular machine learning. We examine how FeatLLM transforms ambiguous textual logic into precise, executable binary features, navigating the inherent noise of generative outputs through a unique meta-promoting paradigm before integrating these features into a lightweight probabilistic model.

## The Challenge of Parsing Noisy Text Outputs from Generative Models

When an LLM is tasked with generating decision rules for tabular data, its primary output modality remains natural language. While powerful for reasoning, this modality introduces significant instability when translated into code or logical predicates required for automated data transformation. Unlike traditional compilers or parsers that expect rigid syntax, an LLM operates on probabilistic token prediction. Consequently, even when strictly instructed to adhere to a specific format regarding conditional statements (e.g., specifying thresholds or category memberships), the model exhibits considerable variance in expression. One iteration might express a condition as `$age > 50$`, while another might phrase it identically in intent but differently in syntax as "`the patient's age is greater than fifty`," or simply "`older than 50 years`."

This syntactic heterogeneity poses a formidable barrier to automated implementation. Traditional hard-coded regular expression parsers fail catastrophically in this environment because they rely on exact string matching patterns that cannot account for the infinite permutations of human-like phrasing generated by an LLM attempting to satisfy a constraint. If a system relies solely on brittle pattern matching, minor deviations—such as swapping "and" for "&", changing capitalization, or reordering clauses—result in parsing failures or silent errors where invalid logic passes unnoticed. In the context of few-shot learning, where every extracted feature carries disproportionate weight toward model convergence, losing valid rules due to parsing ambiguities severely degrades performance. Furthermore, attempts to build increasingly complex custom parsers to handle every conceivable variation introduce their own overhead and brittleness, defeating the purpose of automating the feature engineering pipeline. Therefore, the central challenge is not merely translating symbols but interpreting semantic equivalence amidst syntactic chaos without sacrificing execution speed or reliability.

## Meta-Prompting Code Generation: Leveraging LLMs to Write Executable Python Functions

FeastLLM resolves the parsing dilemma not by constructing better static regex engines, but by recursively deploying the LLM's own capabilities—a technique referred to here as meta-prompting for code generation. Rather than asking humans or static scripts to translate English logic into Python code manually during development-time configuration, FeatLLM treats translation as an inference-time task delegated back to the LLM itself. Since modern foundation models are trained on vast corpora containing programming languages alongside natural language text, they possess an implicit understanding of programming semantics that transcends surface-level lexical variations. The framework leverages this dual competence by prompting the LLM with its own previously generated rules and instructing it to synthesize a corresponding Python function that implements said logic deterministically.

In practice, this involves structuring a secondary prompt that explicitly defines the signature of a function expected from the model: accepting raw row data as input and returning a boolean integer (`0` or `1`) based on whether the row satisfies specific conditions derived from earlier stages of reasoning. This prompt includes the list of generated natural language rules alongside specifications for variable naming conventions consistent with the dataset schema (e.g., mapping "patient_age" from text to `row['age']`). By framing this request within a coding context and emphasizing safety and correctness in instructions similar to Chain-of-Thought reasoning tailored for software synthesis ("Let's think step-by-step"), guiding words like "Write safe python code" trigger specific behaviors aligned with coding competencies stored in attention heads dedicated to programming tasks. Because this occurs dynamically at runtime based on actual outputs rather than hardcoded templates beforehand—it allows immediate correction—if initial attempt fails compilation checks—which triggers retry mechanisms built-in loop until successful runnable snippet produced finally passed security sandbox check executing efficiently inside isolated interpreter session avoiding injection attacks # Chapter 5: Downstream Modeling and Ensemble Strategies

Having established how Large Language Models (LLMs) act as expert feature engineers to derive semantically meaningful rules from tabular data, we must now address the critical phase of transforming these linguistic artifacts into quantitative predictors. The core innovation of the FeatLLM framework lies not solely in the generation of these rules, but in the sophisticated downstream pipeline that converts them into robust probabilistic classifications. Once an LLM produces a set of natural language rules—conditions determining whether a sample belongs to a specific class—the system must parse these strings into executable logic, transform raw data into binary indicators, and finally fit a predictive model capable of weighing the evidence provided by each rule. This chapter delves deep into the mechanics of this downstream modeling process. We will explore why a seemingly simple linear architecture is sufficient when paired with high-quality engineered features, examine the mathematical necessity of constraining weights to ensure monotonic contribution, and dissect how ensemble methodologies, specifically combining bagging with stochastic rule generation, serve as the primary defense against hallucinated rules and hardware-imposed context limits. Understanding this pipeline is essential for grasping how FeatLLM achieves state-of-the-art performance while maintaining computational efficiency comparable to classical tabular learning algorithms.

### The Linear Classifier Objective Function with Non-Negative Weight Constraints

Once the LLM has successfully inferred a series of discriminative rules for each class category, the immediate challenge is to quantify their collective influence on the final decision boundary. Unlike end-to-end deep learning approaches where hidden layers implicitly learn feature interactions through millions of parameters, FeatLLM decouples representation learning from classification. The former is handled entirely by the LLM's vast pre-trained knowledge base, leaving the latter to a transparent, interpretable statistical model. Specifically, FeatLLM employs a linear model devoid of bias terms to map the newly constructed binary feature vectors onto class logits. If we denote the generated binary feature vector derived from sample $\mathbf{x}^i$ for a specific class $k$ as $\mathbf{z}_k^i \in \{0, 1\}^R$, where $R$ represents the fixed number of rules extracted per class (typically ten), the transformation relies fundamentally on a dot-product operation between these features and a set of trainable scalar weights $\mathbf{w}_k$.

The design choice to utilize such a parsimonious linear model is driven by several factors rooted in both theory and practice. Firstly, because the upstream LLM has already performed heavy lifting regarding non-linear transformations and contextual understanding—encoding complex dependencies between features and labels into discrete boolean flags—a simple linear combination is theoretically adequate to aggregate this signal. Secondly, and perhaps more critically in low-shot regimes where labeled data is scarce ($k < N$), complex non-linear models prone to capturing noise are highly susceptible to severe overfitting. A linear model minimizes hypothesis space complexity, forcing reliance on the quality of the engineered features themselves rather than fitting idiosyncrasies in tiny training sets. However, simply applying ordinary least squares or unconstrained logistic regression introduces risks inherent to automated feature engineering: what happens if an incorrectly generated rule negatively correlates with its intended target? To counteract this instability, FeatLLM imposes strict structural constraints on its optimization landscape by enforcing non-negativity on all trainable weights $\mathbf{w}_k$. This constraint dictates that every rule generated by the LLM contributes monotonically toward increasing or holding constant the predicted probability for its associated class; no single rule can ever actively decrease the likelihood of membership upon being triggered (i.e., evaluating to true). Mathematically, before computing the dot product with any incoming activation pattern $\mathbf{z}_k^i$, each element of $\mathbf{w}_k$ undergoes projection onto a strictly positive subspace via a rectification mechanism:

$$
\hat{\mathbf{w}}_k = \max(\mathbf{w}_k, 0)
$$

This operation ensures that regardless of initial gradient updates or fluctuations during early training stages where signs might momentarily flip due to sparse gradients or numerical instabilities, only positive magnitudes contribute to final calculations. Conceptually aligns well with domain intuition regarding diagnostic heuristics: satisfying more medical symptoms generally raises suspicion rather than lowering it unless explicitly framed as exclusionary criteria—which here are captured by separate negative-consequence rules assigned distinct indices within another class grouping if necessary—but primarily acts as insurance against adversarial behaviors arising from imperfect prompt adherence or misinterpreted semantics during rule synthesis phases orchestrated earlier in our workflow pipeline today! Thus achieving stability guarantees even under extreme scarcity conditions typical few-shot scenarios encountered throughout industry deployments worldwide right now essentially speaking anyway
